You reported a power analysis that has been classified as ‘post-hoc’. Calculating observed power is almost never useful. If you actually performed a sensitivity power analysis, label it as such explicitly.

power_id text
1 This pre-registered study (https://osf.io/yufqs) was conducted via the Prolific platform in 2024. Because there has been no prior work on the present research question, we conducted a power analysis to ensure 80% power to detect a small effect (Cohen’s d = 0.2). This test, implemented with the “pwr.t.test” (Champely, 2020) function in R (R Core Team, 2026), indicated that 198 participants were required. We pre-registered an inflated target N of 250 to provide additional power to detect effects that may have been smaller in size. An initial set of participants (N = 674) completed a screener that assessed inclusion criteria for the study.
2;3;4;5 Note that the sample size of sequential bilinguals(n = 196) used in this test is smaller than the N we pre-registered in our power analysis to have 80% power to detect effects as large as d = .20 (i.e., n = 198). That said, it is only 2 participants short from this target, and power analyses show that this sample grants 79.6% power to detect small effects (d = .20) and 80% power to detect effects as small as d = .201. Notably, the effect size we do observe for this effect (d = .07; a within-person shift of seven one-hundredths of a standard deviation) is far smaller than this effect size of interest, and the average difference in reappraisal success we observe is a numerical shift of 0.7 points on a one-hundred point scale. In fact, if the population effect is truly d = .07, that would require 1,604 participants to detect with 80% power.
6 This analysis returned main effects of both Audio Content, F(1, 13524) = 1432.96, p < .001, ηp² = .10, 90% CI [.09, .11], and Language, F(1, 13524) = 11.14, p < .001, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, .002], but no interaction between them, F(1, 13524) = 0.66, p = .416, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, < .001]. To provide a common-sense estimate of the impact of Language on reappraisal within this subsample, we computed a Cohen’s d effect size for the difference in reappraisal efficacy across Spanish and English conditions. We computed participant-level mean differences in ratings for Spanish Describe and Spanish Reappraise trials, as well as participant-level mean differences in ratings for English Describe and English Reappraise trials, and then we measured the size of the within-subjects difference across these two estimates of “reappraisal success” in each language. This difference was very small (d = 0.07, 95% CI [-0.07, 0.21]), with reappraisal success numerically larger in English (M = 15.01) than Spanish (M = 14.31). Thus, if there is any effect within this subsample, it is extremely small (seven one-hundredths of a standard-deviation change within-persons) and would require 1,604 participants to detect with 80% power. Although this is a post-hoc test in just a subsample of participants (see footnote 3 in the primary article for a discussion of power), this result again aligns with hypothesis 3c: There is no compelling evidence that reappraisal success differs between L1 and L2 in Spanish-English bilinguals, even when restricting to sequential bilinguals only.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Marginal Significance

What we found

You described 1 effect with terms related to ‘marginally significant’.

Show details

You described effects with terms related to ‘marginally significant’. If p values above 0.05 are interpreted as an effect, you inflate the alpha level, and increase the Type 1 error rate. If a p value is higher than the prespecified alpha level, it should be interpreted as a non-significant result.

Text Section Header
There was no significant effect of Language in the condition-level ANOVA, though the result was on the border of significance, F(1, 249) = 3.72, p = .055, ηp² = .01, 90% CI [.00, .05]. V. Pre-registered Analyses of Condition-Level Averages

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate at which non-significant p-values are interpreted as marginally significant, see:

Olsson-Collentine A, van Assen MALM, Hartgerink CHJ (2019). “The Prevalence of Marginally Significant Results in Psychology Over Time.” Psychological Science, 30, 576–586. doi:10.1177/0956797619830326.

For the list of terms used to identifify marginally significant results, see this blog post by Matthew Hankins.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 4 imprecise p values out of 24 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
ps > .837 T-tests for all pairwise comparisons produced ps > .837, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Table S3 for values).
ps > .130 All t-tests produced ps > .130, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Tables S2 and S4 for details).
p ≤ .05 All uncorrected p-values that do not survive correction are.01 ≤ p ≤ .05 and their effects are extremely small (i.e., ηp² ≤ .001, save for one moderator main effect that is larger).
p < .05 Table S6: Statistics for moderator interactions with uncorrected p < .05.

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 10 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Text Sentence
ps > .837 T-tests for all pairwise comparisons produced ps > .837, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Table S3 for values).
ps > .130 All t-tests produced ps > .130, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Tables S2 and S4 for details).

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. setwd() calls were found in 1 code file.

File name setwd() calls found
DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd setwd(‘~/Google Drive/Shared drives/LEmoLab/LEmoLab/Projects_Active/Cao_Bilingual/[Study] Data Files + Code/Data_OSF’)

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In all code files, libraries/imports were loaded in one block.

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 15 distinct packages/libraries: devtools, dplyr, effects, effectsize, emmeans, ez, ggplot2, introdataviz, knitr, lmerTest, MBESS, psych, pwr, reshape2, Rmisc. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 119 columns across 3 data files.

Issues Identified

13 file/column combinations have at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

Careless Responding

78 distinct respondents were flagged for straightlining: giving the same answer for at least 80% of the items in a multi-item scale of 5 items or more. One respondent can be flagged in several scales; the table below is one row per person, and the Longest run column gives the run that triggered the strongest flag, so you can check it against the raw data.

Of these, 49 were flagged only by short-scale straightlining (a run of identical answers on a scale of 7 items or fewer). On a short, one-directional scale, answering consistently is often normal, coherent responding rather than carelessness — treat these as weak signals and inspect the actual responses before excluding anyone.

These are prompts to inspect those rows, not definitive judgements.

What this does not cover. Only 1 of 3 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A package could not be installed.

  • DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd needs introdataviz.
  • DataAnalysis_R2.R needs introdataviz.

introdataviz could not be installed with the current version of R, probably because it is old. Record the versions you used (in R, renv::init() writes them to a renv.lock file) and your R version. To be sure the code keeps running, provide a Docker image: a frozen copy of the whole computer setup the code needs, which you can build from rocker/r-ver at your R version.

Other problems in the files

  • DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd calls setwd(), which ties the script to a folder on the author’s computer. We replaced the call with one that does nothing so the script could run; it should be removed from the script.

We found no statistical tests in the paper to compare with the output of the code.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

This module assesses whether the paper’s code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 2 R code files.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first), source() calls, and numeric filename prefixes.

1 DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd — numeric
2 DataAnalysis_R2.R DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd dependency

File paths

Every data path in the code works in the repository as deposited.

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 2 scripts: 0 ran without error, 0 errored, 0 timed out, 2 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions. 1 script uses an object or function that only another script defines, so in the second run that script was loaded first, in the same session: DataAnalysis_R2.R after DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd. Such a script should source() the other itself, since it does not run on its own.

a script hit an object '...' not found error, indicating it expected a variable another script defines. We inferred which script supplies it and re-ordered so that script runs first, and re-ran once. The outcomes above are from that corrected run.

Dependency-unavailable errors

2 scripts could not run because their own dependency is unavailable — the package failed to install from both live CRAN and the CRAN Archive (or a GitHub/URL source could not be resolved). This is an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so it does not force the traffic light red.

File
DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd
DataAnalysis_R2.R

setwd() in the code

1 script calls setwd(). This is bad practice: it hardcodes where the code must run (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine) and breaks the moment the code is run anywhere else. To run the code we replaced these calls with a statement that does nothing. Any such script that then ran is reproducible only after ignoring a setwd() that should not have been in the code and needs to be fixed.

File setwd() removed Path(s) it set
DataAnalysis_R2.Rmd 1 ~/Google Drive/Shared drives/LEmoLab/LEmoLab/Projects_Active/Cao_Bilingual/[Study] Data Files + Code/Data_OSF

Calls that install packages in the code

2 calls to install.packages(), groundhog, checkpoint, renv, pacman and the like were replaced, since they install packages themselves: groundhog.library() and p_load() by library(), the others by a statement that does nothing. The scripts ran with the packages installed before the run.

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

In 2024, we recruited 250 U.S.-based Spanish-English bilingual women who reported learning Spanish before (78.4%) or concurrently with English (21.6%) and demonstrated high Spanish and English proficiency.

Participants were presented with negative images before hearing audio recordings either describing the negative contents of the image or reinterpreting those contents to be less negative (i.e., social reappraisal) in either Spanish or English.

Third, Quiñones-Camacho et al. (2019) found that Spanish-English bilinguals reported using cognitive reappraisal more in English (L2) than in Spanish (L1) and that the impact of emotion regulation on physiology depended on the language participants were speaking.

This pre-registered study (https://osf.io/yufqs) was conducted via the Prolific platform in 2024.

This test, implemented with the “pwr.t.test” (Champely, 2020) function in R (R Core Team, 2026), indicated that 198 participants were required.

Pre-registered inclusion criteria included: living in the United States, a 95% or higher approval rating on previous studies (calculated by Prolific), being 18-39 years old, identifying as women, reporting Spanish as an earliest learned language -all of which were prescreened by Prolificand passing an auditory language translation quiz that ensured adequate proficiency to comprehend study materials (see Language Experience and Proficiency Screener below).

Participants were first screened for language experience via Prolific where they responded to the following question: “What language(s) did you speak earliest in life?”

Participants completed a subset of questions from the Language Experience and Proficiency Questionnaire (LEAP-Q) in English, which is a validated tool for collecting self-reported proficiency and language history (Kaushanskaya et al., 2020; Marian et al., 2007).

As part of this assessment, participants provided a list of languages they knew in order of dominance and order of acquisition, language proficiency ratings, and age of immigration to the U.S. (if applicable).

Only participants who received a perfect score on this translation quiz (74% of people who completed the quiz) were invited through Prolific to participate in the full study.

Participants completed the emotion regulation task and questionnaires via Gorilla (https://gorilla.sc/), an online experiment builder for implementing behavioral tasks and surveys.

Before beginning the study, participants completed a CAPTCHA attention check (i.e., writing visually degraded numbers into text boxes) and audio check (i.e., hearing the word “decimal” and writing the word into a text box).

Participants completed a social reappraisal task adapted from prior research (Sahi et al., 2021 (Sahi et al.,, 2025)).

After viewing the image and listening to the clip, participants had 4 seconds to rate how bad they felt on a sliding scale ranging from 0 to 100.

Before beginning the task, participants were asked to select where 0 and 100 were represented on the scale.

Participants were warned that they would have limited time to make their rating, and an image of a red square would appear at the bottom of the affect rating scale if they were too slow.

Participants completed 72 trials of this task (i.e., 18 trials per condition).

To minimize any potential language-switching costs in auditory comprehension (Olson, 2017), trials were grouped into Spanish and English blocks such that participants completed all Spanish trials contiguously and all English trials contiguously.

Trials were auto-advancing, and we included two attention checks randomly interspersed through the task in which participants had to drag a specified number from a list of numbers into a dropzone and submit.

A pilot version of the study was conducted on Prolific with a separate sample of 12 participants to ensure that reappraisals produced lower negative affect than descriptions across both languages before proceeding to collect data for the main study, F(1, 715.20) = 73.16, p < .001, ηp² = .09, 90% CI [.06, .13].

After the task, participants completed a set of questionnaires in English assessing demographics and relevant traits that could moderate our effects.

Participants completed this 8-item measure of depressive symptoms over the last two weeks (e.g., “feeling down, depressed, or hopeless;” Kroenke et al., 2009).

The primary pre-registered outcome measures for each participant were their negative affect ratings averaged within the task’s four conditions: Spanish Describe, Spanish Reappraise, English Describe, and English Reappraise.

  1. German-French (n=20) and French-German (n=21) 41 German-French Bilinguals: Intensity = 5.2, French-German Bilinguals: Intensity = 4.3 German-French Bilinguals: Proficiency = 74.5/ 100, AoA = 10.8, Intensity = 3.4, French-German Bilinguals: Proficiency = 72.4/100, AoA = 12.3, Intensity = 4.8 Affective priming effects were consistently found in L1, but only emerged in L2 when participants had high L2 language use and immersion.

Note, to access the study at all, participants must have answered “Spanish” to Prolific’s demographics question regarding “What language(s) did you speak earliest in life?”, providing initial filtering to ensure Spanish was one of our participants’ earliest languages.

For thoroughness, we utilized additional measures that we had collected that were relevant to this research question (L1 age of acquisition, whether participants were simultaneous bilinguals [reported learning English concurrently with Spanish] or sequential bilinguals [reported learning English after Spanish], trait use of cognitive reappraisal in daily life, depression symptoms, and anxiety symptoms).

Age of acquisition and proficiency for each language, as well as dominance and whether participants were simultaneous or sequential bilinguals were all assessed using the adapted LEAP-Q presented in Supplemental Section I (proficiency is the average of the reading, speaking, and understanding items).

Although this exclusion criterion was not pre-registered, adding it provides additional confidence that participants were attentive and honest in their reporting.

  1. Spanish-English 274 Native fluency Summer intensive course grades = 8.14/10 Participants were more accurate in judging emotion expressions in L2 but inferred the expressor was experiencing greater emotional intensity in L1.

Notes: a These participants were included in the study because they reported on the Prolific prescreener that they identified as women, but they identified otherwise in our demographics.

We conducted a non-pre-registered analysis testing whether the order in which participants completed English versus Spanish blocks affected their responses.

Thus, the main effects of Content and Language were not attributable to which language participants completed first.

However, the lack of a three-way interaction of Content × Language × Language Block Order indicates that the main effects of Content and Language were not attributable to which language participants completed first.

We added this for additional certainty that participants were attentive and honest.

Ethics approval statement:

All methods were approved by the Princeton University Institutional Review Board (IRB #15744: “Examining Differences in Cognitive Reappraisal Efficacy for Spanish-English Bilinguals”).

Preregistration Check

We found 1 preregistration.

id title template
yufqs Examining differences in social reappraisal efficacy across first- and second-language in Spanish-English bilinguals OSF Preregistration

Meta-scientific research has shown that deviations from preregistrations are often not reported or checked, and that the most common deviations concern the sample size. We recommend manually checking the full preregistration at the link above, and have provided the preregistered sample size.

id sample_size
yufqs We aim to have a final sample of 250 usable participants for our primary analysis.
Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title Examining differences in social reappraisal efficacy across first- and second-language in Spanish-English bilinguals
id yufqs
link https://osf.io/yufqs
date_created 2024-02-20T22:52:39.885829
date_modified 2024-02-20T22:51:17.128226
date_registered 2024-02-20T22:52:39.858311
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-yufqs-v1
research_questions Bilingualism — the use of two languages in everyday life — is an increasingly common linguistic experience for children worldwide (Byers-Heinlein et al., 2019). Bilingual children receive input from both their first language chronologically acquired (L1) and their second language (L2) as they begin to learn about emotions and emotion regulation. Some studies on adult bilinguals have found that emotional response to stimuli (self-reported or measured physiologically) is more intense when presented in L1 compared to in L2 (Chen et al., 2012; Dylman & Bjärtå, 2018; Harris, 2004; Harris et al., 2003, 2006). However, we know little about how emotion regulation differs in L1 vs. L2 for bilinguals. Emotion regulation refers to the processes by which individuals influence how they experience and express emotions (Gross, 1998). To date, scholars have shown that self-reported distress decreases when Swedish (L1)-English (L2) bilinguals switched from reading in L1 to answering questions in L2, consistent with prior work showing reduced intensity of emotions in L2 than L1 (Dylman & Bjärtå, 2018). Additionally, Spanish-English bilinguals reported using cognitive reappraisal (i.e., changing one’s interpretation of a stimulus to change one’s emotions), more often in English than in Spanish (Quiñones-Camacho et al., 2019), suggesting that people may choose more “cognitive” emotion regulation strategies in L2 than L1 contexts.

However, studies have yet to experimentally compare how the efficacy of the same emotion regulation strategy differs in a bilingual person’s first or second language. Testing this research question can further our understanding of how the bilingual experience affects emotional learning and processing, with implications for how we can provide better emotional education and support to bilingual populations. The current study manipulates the language in which Spanish-English bilinguals hear cognitive reappraisals of negative images and examines if one language is more effective than the other (Spanish vs. English) at alleviating (self-reported) negative affect. Recruited participants will be aged 18–39, based in the United States, with a history of learning Spanish first and English second (or concurrently). Due to possible neural differences in the use of cognitive reappraisal between genders (McRae et al., 2008; Sahi et al., 2023), only participants who identify as women will be recruited. Participants will have to pass a Spanish-English translation quiz to ensure sufficient proficiency in both languages before moving on to the formal study. Demographics, language history data, and self-reported language proficiency levels will also be collected.

Our primary hypotheses are as follows:
1) Participants will on average report more negative affect on Describe trials compared to Reappraise trials, regardless of language. This finding would replicate prior work on the effect of cognitive reappraisal in reducing negative emotions.
2) Participants will on average report more negative affect in Spanish trials compared to English trials, regardless of the contents of the audio clips (i.e., Describe or Reappraise). This finding would replicate previous research findings of L1 producing more intense emotional responses than L2.
3) We do not have a clear prediction regarding the interaction of language and audio contents; there are three possible alternative hypotheses:
3.1) Participants may show greater cognitive reappraisal efficacy in L1 than in L2. If so, this would imply that regulation is more effective in Spanish than in English due to greater fluency and/or familiarity with reappraisal and emotion regulation in the language they learned first.
3.2) Participants may show greater cognitive reappraisal efficacy in L2 than in L1. If so, this would imply that regulation is more effective in English than in Spanish due to increased emotional distance (i.e., reduced intensity of emotions) in L2.
3.3) No interaction may emerge. This would imply that cognitive reappraisal efficacy is equivalent in L1 and L2.
study_type Experiment - A researcher randomly assigns treatments to study subjects, this includes field or lab experiments. This is also known as an intervention experiment and includes randomized controlled trials.
blinding No blinding is involved in this study.
study_design_overview The study deploys a 2 [Language: Spanish vs. English] x 2 [Audio Content: Describe vs. Reappraise] within-subject paradigm adapted from a previous study on cognitive reappraisal (Sahi et al., 2021). Participants will view 72 negative images evenly distributed across the four resulting conditions. To minimize the need to switch between languages, trials will be grouped into Spanish and English blocks, with the order of these blocks counterbalanced across participants. Within each block, the order of “Describe” and “Reappraise” trials is randomized. “Describe” trials will have participants listening to factual descriptions of the negative images, and “Reappraise” trials will have participants listening to positive cognitive reappraisals of the images. Following each trial, participants will rate their negative affect on a scale of 0–100 (0 = “not bad at all”; 100 = “very bad”). Note that the task instructions are explained in English for all subjects, but the rating scale is presented without any words (just 0–100) so it is equivalent across Spanish and English blocks. We will analyze negative affect ratings in each condition to determine whether cognitive reappraisal is more effective in Spanish or English at reducing participants’ negative affect. To ensure condition differences are attributable to our task’s factors (and not specific images or audio recordings), we have created 4 matched lists of images each with 4 matched sets of audio recordings (i.e., English Describe, English Reappraise, Spanish Describe, and Spanish Reappraise), and we will counterbalance participants to image/audio set.
existing_data Registration prior to creation of data
data_collection_procedures The target demographic will be 18–39 year-old, Spanish (L1) and English (L2 or concurrent L1) bilingual speakers who identify as a woman, based in the United States. People outside of this demographic will be excluded. Participants will be recruited from the online platform Prolific, must have a 95% or higher approval rating on previous studies (calculated by Prolific) to be included. Screener participants will be excluded if they fail to pass the language translation quiz that ensures a minimum proficiency level in Spanish and English to comprehend study materials. Participants who do pass the screener will be invited within a week to take part in the study trials; all communication will be through the platform Prolific.
sample_size We aim to have a final sample of 250 usable participants for our primary analysis.
sample_size_rationale Because prior work has never tested this specific comparison, we conducted our power analyses using a small Cohen’s d of 0.2. Using the “pwr.t.test” in R (R Core Team, 2022), we found that 198 participants were required for 80% power to detect a main effect of d = 0.2. We inflated this target N to 250 to provide additional power to assess interaction and other effects including the exploratory moderations described below.
stopping_rule Given that exclusion criteria may lead us to exclude participants after they have participated, we will recruit participants in waves. We will aim to have 250 usable participants but the size of the final wave and proportion we have to exclude may lead to a small number more than this target. But we will stop when the last wave provides at least 250 usable participants.
manipulated_variables There are four conditions in total: a “Describe” condition and a “Reappraise” condition in two languages, Spanish and English. Participants in this within-subjects design will have trials of “Describe” and “Reappraise” conditions in both Spanish and English. The “Describe” trials have participants listening to audio clips factually describing the contents of the image stimuli, with the audio in either Spanish or English. The “Reappraise” trials will involve audio clips of cognitive reappraisals of the image stimuli. The order of the Spanish and English blocks will be counterbalanced across participants, while the order of “Describe” and “Reappraise” trials in each block will be randomized for each participant. The stimuli used in the four groups will be distributed such that average valence and arousal ratings of the images do not differ significantly between conditions, and assignment of stimulus list to condition is counterbalanced across participants.
measured_variables There is one primary dependent variable from the emotion regulation task: negative affect ratings. Participants will rate their negative affect on a slider, in response to seeing negative images and hearing audio clips that either describe the image factually or reappraise the image. The slider will have a scale of 0–100 (0 = “not bad at all”, 100 = “very bad”). There will be a total of 72 ratings for each participant, and 18 per condition (2 [Language: Spanish vs. English] x 2 [Audio Content: Describe vs. Reappraise]). We will average these ratings per condition.

Other key variables assess Spanish and English language proficiency and dominance. We will also collect participants’ self-reported proficiency, where they will rate their level of proficiency in speaking, understanding (listening), and reading Spanish and English each on a scale of 0–10 (0 = “none”, 10 = “perfect”). The three scores for each language will be averaged for an overall proficiency score. Considering this study’s usage of audio clips, the understanding (listening) scores in Spanish and English specifically may also be separately used in exploratory analyses.

We will collect additional data from participants: (1) age of acquisition of L1 and L2, (2) languages ranked in order of dominance, and (3) questionnaire data (e.g., Bilingual Switching Questionnaire, Emotion Regulation Questionnaire, Patient Health Questionnaire 8, and Generalized Anxiety Disorder 8). In particular, the Bilingual Switching Questionnaire measures participants’ tendencies to switch from one language to another (switch to L1 and switch to L2). Earlier acquisition and/or greater proficiency in one language is correlated with fewer switches to the other language (Rodriguez-Fornells et al., 2012). We collect these data for exploratory follow-up analyses. We will transparently report in the final paper that analyses of these DVs are exploratory in nature.
statistical_tests Analyses will be conducted using R. We will test our primary hypotheses by performing a 2 × 2 ANOVA to quantify how Language (L1 vs. L2) and Audio Content (Describe vs. Reappraise) factors affect negative affect ratings. We will follow-up a significant interaction of language and audio content with condition-wise t-tests.
inference_criteria Our alpha level is set to .05, and tests will be two-tailed.
data_exclusion_criteria Data from participants who fail the CAPTCHA bot check or both attention checks (there are two, one in the Spanish block and one in the English block) will be excluded. If participants only fail one attention check, we will do a qualitative check on their responses to open-ended questions (i.e., mandatory feedback form) to evaluate their attentiveness before deciding to exclude them. In addition, given that the trials auto-advance, we will exclude any participant who fails to respond to more than 50% of trials in any condition. Finally, participants who leave midway through the study due to language comprehension issues or psychological discomfort will be compensated for their time but will be excluded from data analysis.
missing_data_handling Within the task, we will compute average ratings within each condition. Missing trials will be ignored in these averages (though we exclude anyone who does not respond to more than 50% of trials in a condition).

If participants do not provide data for supplementary analyses (e.g., proficiency questions), we will exclude them only from analyses of those specific data.
exploratory_analyses We will explore using measures of proficiency, age of L2 acquisition, self-reported language dominance, and language-switching frequency scores as possible moderators in the ANOVA. For example, we can test whether the benefits of L1 vs L2 reappraisal vary depending on whether participants report English or Spanish as their currently ‘dominant’ language by adding it as a factor in the ANOVA and testing for a 3-way interaction in the ANOVA: 2 [Language] x 2 [Content] x 2 [Dominance]. Similarly, participants can be divided into two groups based on age of L2 acquisition (before the age of 12 [i.e. early to middle childhood] and after 12); three groups based on proficiency (L1 > L2, L1 = L2, and L1 < L2); and three groups based on language-switching frequency (more switches to L1, equal frequency of switching, and more switches to L2). If there are significant interactions, we will follow up with more fine-grained analyses to unpack what is driving the effect.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

:::

Full text

Abstract

People frequently turn to others for help managing their emotions. This process of social emotion regulation often unfolds via language: For example, support providers can verbally offer new perspectives that shift another person's emotions. Inspired by research showing that emotional reactivity is lower in a bilingual's second language (L2) than their first language (L1), we tested whether social emotion regulation was more effective in Spanish (L1) or English (L2) for Spanish-English bilinguals. We pre-registered countervailing hypotheses, as regulation could either be more effective in L1 (due to increased familiarity and fluency), L2 (due to decreased emotional intensity), or no different across languages. In 2024, we recruited 250 U.S.-based Spanish-English bilingual women who reported learning Spanish before (78.4%) or concurrently with English (21.6%) and demonstrated high Spanish and English proficiency. Participants were presented with negative images before hearing audio recordings either describing the negative contents of the image or reinterpreting those contents to be less negative (i.e., social reappraisal) in either Spanish or English. Participants reported lower negative affect overall on English than Spanish trials, extending prior work demonstrating reduced emotional intensity in L2.

Interestingly, social reappraisals lowered participants' negative affect in both languages, with no difference across languages. Thus, Spanish-English bilinguals like those tested here may equally benefit from social reappraisal in both languages. Results advance basic research on language and emotion, guide translational applications for this population, and prompt future research (e.g., identifying moderators or examining other bilingual groups).

[div-01]

Emotion regulation, the processes through which people influence how they experience and express emotions (Gross, 1998), is robustly associated with well-being (Aldao et al., 2010; Berking et al., 2008; DeSteno et al., 2013). As social creatures, our strategies for regulating emotions often involve interactions with other people: Emotional experiences stimulate us to share emotions with others and render seeking social support as one of the most prevalent responses to negative emotional events (Rimé, 2009; Zaki & Williams, 2013). Although there are many strategies for regulating emotions (Gross, 1998 (Gross, , 2015)), researchers often focus on reappraisal (i.e., changing how one thinks about or "appraises" a situation to change its emotional impact), given its potency in changing emotion and its similarity to widely used forms of psychotherapy, like Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (Beck, 1995). Interestingly, cognitive reappraisal can be deployed both individually on one's own or in a social setting, where another person's perspective changes how we think and feel. In fact, recent research has demonstrated that cognitive reappraisal is more effective when a friend helps us compared to regulating on our own (Sahi et al., 2021 (Sahi et al., , 2025)).

Language, which plays an important role in the experience and perception of emotions (Barrett, 2006; Barrett et al., 2025; Hoemann et al., 2019; Lindquist et al., 2015; Nook, Sasse, et al., 2017), is a core medium for communication during social emotion regulation. In the age of globalization, children are increasingly being raised in multi-lingual environments, where their social interactions across a day or week can take place in a number of different languages (Boyer, 2012; Chen et al., 2012; Williams et al., 2020). The growing prevalence of bilingualism has motivated research into the effects of the bilingual experience on multiple facets of human development, including emotional experiences.
