Previous studies have repeatedly found a bilingual's second language (L2) to be less "emotionally resonant" than their first language (L1) on a variety of measures. Reduced affective intensity or arousal, has been shown across self-reported ratings of affect and stimuli (Chen et al., 2012; Dewaele, 2004; Dylman & Bjärtå, 2019), physiological arousal as measured by skin conductance response (García-Palacios et al., 2018; Harris, 2004; Harris et al., 2006), speed of processing emotion words (Sheikh & Titone, 2016), and even brain activity (Davydova et al., 2026). For example, one study had Swedish-English bilinguals read negative-emotion-provoking texts in either L1 Swedish or L2 English and answer questions about the text in either language (Dylman & Bjärtå, 2019).

Participants who read the texts in L1 Swedish and then answered questions in L2 English reported lower ratings of distress compared to participants who both read the text and answered questions in L1. Seemingly, the switch from the more emotional L1 to the less emotional L2 produced a decrease in distress. Similarly, physiological and neural responses to the threat of shock are reduced when processing stimuli in L2 than in L1 (Davydova et al., 2026; García-Palacios et al., 2018).

Interestingly, some studies show that the extent to which L1 emotional reactivity differs from that of L2 varies depending on the types of stimuli used, the context being studied, and one's language background factors, such as age of L2 acquisition and language proficiencies (see Table 1 for a summary of related studies and participant characteristics). In particular, studies seem to suggest that the linguistic contexts in which bilinguals learned emotion concepts may affect the extent to which their emotional response differs in L1 versus L2. For example, Turkish-English bilinguals had significantly stronger physiological responses to reprimands ("Don't do that!") in L1 Turkish than in L2 English, but this difference was not found for other categories of words (neutral [door], positive [bride], aversive [disease], or taboo [breast]). This difference may arise because reprimands were more relevant to participants' childhood when they first started learning language and emotional expressions (Harris et al., 2003). Indeed, parents in bilingual households report using the mother tongue (i.e., L1) more often than other languages for emotion regulation and teaching cultural and moral values to their children, and studies have linked L2-L1 language switches during parent-child conversations to the valence of parent facial expressions (Boyer, 2012; Chen et al., 2012; Williams et al., 2020).

Relatedly, the cultural norms associated with a language can shape bilinguals' emotion concepts and influence their emotional reactivity and use of emotional language (Mesquita & Frijda, 1992): For bilinguals whose L1 culture doesn't encourage direct expression of praise and affection, like Chinese or Finnish, they report being more comfortable verbalizing such emotions in their L2 English (Caldwell-Harris et al., 2011; Chen et al., 2012; Williams et al., 2020).

Studies have also found that the relative learning history and use of each language matters. Differences in physiological reactions for L1 reprimands emerged in "sequential" bilinguals who learned L2 after L1 but not in "simultaneous" bilinguals who learned both concurrently (Harris, 2004), affective priming effects for both L1 and L2 valenced words emerged only for bilinguals with strong L2 use (Degner et al., 2012), and differences in neural responses to threat-conditioning paradigms across L1 and L2 emerged in "unbalanced" bilinguals (who rarely used L2) but not "balanced" bilinguals (who used both languages; Davydova et al., 2026). These studiesfoot_0 suggest that differences in L1 and L2 affective processing may be most detectable in populations where there is an "imbalance" across languages. Earlier L2 learning or greater L2 use appear to diminish differences in affective processing across languages.

Thus, research so far shows that bilinguals generally respond more strongly to affective stimuli in L1 than L2, but language learning histories and use can moderate this effect. What might this mean for social emotion processing? Do differences in affective reactions across languages also shape social processes in dyadic settings beyond parenting, where these differences could impact key outcomes of social interactions, like how we infer others' emotions or how supported we feel by others? Existing work examining this question primarily focuses on emotion perception, or how bilinguals conceptualize facial expressions of emotion in L1 or L2.

In one study, Matsumoto, Anguas-Wong, et al. (2008) observed that Spanish-English college students in Mexico were more "accurate" at perceiving facial expressions in English than in Spanish, but they inferred that expressers were experiencing more intense emotions in Spanish than English. This finding suggests that decreased emotional resonance in L2 may occur both intrapersonally and interpersonally. Curiously, though, Champoux-Larsson & Nook (2024) found no difference in how strongly L1 or L2 emotion words primed emotion perception in Swedish-English bilinguals. Thus, L1/L2 differences in emotion perception may emerge in self-reported perceptions of others' emotions, not implicit measures of emotion concept priming.

Perhaps most important is the question of how emotion regulation (i.e., explicit attempts to change one's own or others' emotions; Gross, 1998; Petrova & Gross, 2023; Zaki & Williams, 2013) varies in bilingual populations. To our knowledge, only three reports have addressed this question. First, the Matsumoto, Anguas-Wong, et al. (2008) study described above found that participants reported higher emotion regulation abilities in Spanish (L1) than in English (L2).

Interestingly, these differences in emotion regulation scores across languages mediated the language differences in perceived emotion intensity, suggesting that emotion regulation and emotion perception may be intertwined across languages. Second, Lau et al. (2026) conducted six experiments across a wide range of language pairings, all showing that bilinguals will strategically plan to use L2 rather than L1 to discuss embarrassing, taboo, or disgusting topics. Furthermore, they demonstrated that this shift aimed to increase perceived psychological distance and reduce emotional or social discomforts. This study thus builds on Dylman & Bjärtå (2019), showing that bilinguals strategically use L2 to down-regulate emotions. Third, Quiñones-Camacho et al. (2019) found that Spanish-English bilinguals reported using cognitive reappraisal more in English (L2) than in Spanish (L1) and that the impact of emotion regulation on physiology depended on the language participants were speaking. For participants with high baseline sympathetic system activity, less use of "emotion engagement" regulation strategies (including reappraisal) was associated with lower physiological reactivity only when discussing negative memories in English (L2). By contrast, regulation strategy use was unrelated to physiological activity in Spanish (L1) contexts, suggesting that L1 discussions of emotional memories were emotionally arousing regardless of whether engagement strategies were used.

These findings offer preliminary glimpses into the ways in which bilingualism may influence emotion and emotion regulation in social settings. In L1, bilinguals report overall greater emotion regulation but using reappraisal less. In L2, they perceive others' emotions more accurately but as less intense, they expect to feel less discomfort discussing embarrassing topics, and regulatory strategies appear to influence physiology.

We extend these findings by integrating research on emotion in bilinguals with contemporary research on social emotion regulation (also called interpersonal emotion regulation). Specifically, we ask whether "social reappraisal" (i.e., receiving regulatory reinterpretations of distressing scenarios) is more or less effective for bilinguals when offered in L1 or L2. There are several motivations for addressing this question. On the theoretical level, the bilingual mind is an ideal testbed for studying how language, emotion, cognitive control, and social processes interact. Bilingualism is thought to have broad impacts on cognition, beyond the effects on emotion reviewed above. A host of studies suggest that bilinguals "juggle" two languages that are simultaneously active in the mind (i.e., L1 is active when reading or speaking in L2 and vice versa; Kroll et al., 2012). This simultaneous activation can generate interference of the not-active language that requires additional inhibitory control in the moment, but bilinguals appear to develop improved cognitive regulation from practice "juggling" languages (Kroll et al., 2012; Zirnstein et al., 2018). This makes the basic study of emotion regulation immensely interesting in bilinguals: How do opposing forces of linguistic interference and practice exerting cognitive control affect emotion regulation in this population?

On a more applied level, addressing this question is extremely important for knowing whether social support efforts should be implemented in one's first or second language to be maximally effective. Every day, bilinguals seek social support from peers, teachers, coaches, family, and therapists, but it remains unknown whether social regulation is more, less, or equally effective in L1 or L2 in this population. Based on the studies above, countervailing hypotheses emerge: It is possible that decreased affective resonance, increased cognitive processing, and increased use of reappraisal in L2 may produce more effective emotion regulation in L2. By contrast, the increased fluency, familiarity, and accessibility of emotional processes in L1 may make regulation in this context more effective. Of course, the third alternative is that regulation in L1 and L2 do not differ (potentially due to skillful "juggling" of both languages), which would provide initial evidence that the choice of language neither hinders nor helps social emotion regulation. Thus, addressing whether social emotion regulation differs across languages for this population can advance both theoretical and translational efforts.

Here, we examined whether social reappraisal was more effective for U.S.-based Spanish-English bilinguals in a first language (L1, Spanish) or second language (L2, English). We intentionally chose this population for several reasons. First, in the United States, around one in ten people speak both Spanish and English, and Spanish is the most common non-English language spoken at home (U.S. Census Bureau, 2022, 2023). Nonetheless, Latinos in the U.S.

(who make up 94% of the Spanish-speaking population; U.S. Census Bureau, 2022) are understudied in psychological science (Haack et al., 2014; Liang et al., 2009; Pedersen et al., 2022) and face unique challenges with mental health care (Altarriba & Santiago-Rivera, 1994; Garcia-Joslin et al., 2016; Santiago-Rivera & Altarriba, 2002). Thus, testing these questions in this population can provide important insight into both basic emotional processes and more translational applications for this sizable group of people. Furthermore, U.S.-based Spanish-English bilinguals tend to be highly fluent in both L1 and L2, with a majority (61%) reporting that they speak English "very well" (U.S. Census Bureau, 2023). Sampling a group with high proficiency in both their languages reduces concerns that stark differences in L1 and L2 proficiency would confound language conditions. Thus, this sample allowed us to test how subtle affective associations in L1 versus L2 influence social emotion regulation. Although these aims motivated our sampling of U.S.-based Spanish-English bilinguals, testing just this sample limits inferences about broader bilingual populations, points we elaborate on in the discussion.

In this pre-registered (https://osf.io/yufqs/) study, we adapted the social reappraisal paradigm from Sahi et al. (2021 Sahi et al. ( , 2025) ) and had U.S.-based Spanish-English bilinguals listen to reappraisals of negative stimuli delivered by an experimenter in either Spanish or English.

Inspired by previous research (Table 1), we pre-registered the hypothesis that participants would generally experience reduced negative affect on L2 (English) trials than on L1 (Spanish) trials.

However, the key test concerned whether language moderated the impact of social reappraisals on affect. We pre-registered the three competing hypotheses noted above for the effect of language on reappraisal efficacy: social reappraisal may be a) more effective in L1 than in L2, b) more effective in L2 than in L1, or c) not different across languages.

Methods Participants

This pre-registered study (https://osf.io/yufqs) was conducted via the Prolific platform in 2024. Because there has been no prior work on the present research question, we conducted a power analysis to ensure 80% power to detect a small effect (Cohen's d = 0.2). This test, implemented with the "pwr.t.test" (Champely, 2020) function in R (R Core Team, 2026), indicated that 198 participants were required. We pre-registered an inflated target N of 250 to provide additional power to detect effects that may have been smaller in size. An initial set of participants (N = 674) completed a screener that assessed inclusion criteria for the study.

Pre-registered inclusion criteria included: living in the United States, a 95% or higher approval rating on previous studies (calculated by Prolific), being 18-39 years old, identifying as women, reporting Spanish as an earliest learned language -all of which were prescreened by Prolificand passing an auditory language translation quiz that ensured adequate proficiency to comprehend study materials (see Language Experience and Proficiency Screener below). We constrained gender to women, as prior studies show gender affects the efficacy of social emotion regulation (McRae et al., 2008; Sahi et al., 2021 Sahi et al., , 2025)). Similarly, given that prior studies show differences in both emotion regulation strategy and efficacy between youth, young adults, and older adults (McRae et al., 2012; Scheibe et al., 2015; Shiota & Levenson, 2009; Silvers et al., 2012; Zimmermann & Iwanski, 2014), we restricted our sample to young adults.

Of those who completed the screener, N = 279 participants met inclusion criteria and were invited to complete the study. Twenty-nine participants failed exclusion criteria 2 (n = 19 failed attention checks involving selecting a specific number on the screen, n = 3 reported learning English earlier than Spanish, n = 1 failed to respond to more than 50% of trials, and n = 6 had different birth years in the screener versus in the study). This left a final usable sample of N = 250 participants (see Table 2 for demographics). All methods were approved by the Princeton University Institutional Review Board (IRB #15744: "Examining Differences in Cognitive Reappraisal Efficacy for Spanish-English Bilinguals").

Language Experience and Proficiency

Participants were first screened for language experience via Prolific where they responded to the following question: "What language(s) did you speak earliest in life?" Participants who selected "Spanish" were able to access a screener from our lab consisting of a set of questions assessing age, gender, and language proficiency, as well as a brief audio translation quiz. Note that participants could select multiple responses to this item, so this only filters participants for whom Spanish was one of their earliest languages. This second screener was implemented to provide additional certainty that participants met study inclusion criteria and to collect relevant language learning variables. Participants completed a subset of questions from the Language Experience and Proficiency Questionnaire (LEAP-Q) in English, which is a validated tool for collecting self-reported proficiency and language history (Kaushanskaya et al., 2020; Marian et al., 2007). As part of this assessment, participants provided a list of languages they knew in order of dominance and order of acquisition, language proficiency ratings, and age of immigration to the U.S. (if applicable). This allowed us to only include participants who reported Spanish being a language they learned first. Almost all participants reported learning Spanish in infancy (i.e., before age 3; Table 2), and the majority of the sample (78.4%) learned Spanish prior to English. However, about one fifth (21.6%) of the sample reported having an equivalent age of acquisition for English and Spanish, 92.4% of participants started learning English by age 9, 82.4% considered English to be their dominant language, and 66.8% reported being more proficient in English than Spanish. Consequently, even though Spanish was learned first or concurrently with English for all participants, English proficiency was very high, and the vast majority considered it their dominant language.

To supplement self-reported language proficiency, we also devised a 15-question multiple-choice auditory translation quiz to ensure a minimum proficiency in both English and Spanish that would allow participants to understand the materials used in the study (see Table S1 for quiz materials). We recorded seven English words and eight Spanish words that were present in our actual study materials. All words were recorded by a bilingual member of the research team who identifies as a woman. Recordings were presented alongside 4 possible translations.

Only participants who received a perfect score on this translation quiz (74% of people who completed the quiz) were invited through Prolific to participate in the full study. We chose an auditory rather than written quiz to reduce risk of participants using online translation tools.

Procedure

Participants completed the emotion regulation task and questionnaires via Gorilla (https://gorilla.sc/), an online experiment builder for implementing behavioral tasks and surveys.

Before beginning the study, participants completed a CAPTCHA attention check (i.e., writing visually degraded numbers into text boxes) and audio check (i.e., hearing the word "decimal" and writing the word into a text box). Failure to pass these checks prevented them from continuing on to the study. All task instructions were presented in English.

Emotion regulation task.

Participants completed a social reappraisal task adapted from prior research (Sahi et al., 2021 (Sahi et al., , 2025)). This task had a 2 [Audio Content: Describe vs. Reappraise] × 2 [Language: Spanish vs. English] within-subjects design (Figure 1). Before starting the task, participants received thorough instructions in English and completed 2 practice trials that mimicked the time constraints of the actual trials.

Trials began with a fixation cross for 1 second. Participants then viewed a negative image and heard an audio clip of a woman talking about the image for 6 seconds. This audio clip determined the condition of the trial: The recording either described the negative contents of the image in Spanish (Spanish Describe condition), described the negative contents of the image in English (English Describe condition), reinterpreted the image to make it more positive in Spanish (Spanish Reappraise condition), or reinterpreted the image in English (English Reappraise condition). To be clear, "Describe" clips were normatively "negative" in nature, aimed at paralleling classic tasks in which participants respond naturally to some images to assess natural affective reactivity to negative scenes (Nook, Schleider, et al., 2017; Ochsner et al., 2002). The "Reappraise" clips involved reinterpretations of the images that introduced a more positive perspective. See Stimuli Preparation for details on how we prepared stimuli to ensure conditions were not confounded by normative differences in image or audio intensity.

After viewing the image and listening to the clip, participants had 4 seconds to rate how bad they felt on a sliding scale ranging from 0 to 100. We removed all language from the affect rating scale so that it would be identical in both Spanish and English conditions. The scale showed an image of bars that grew increasingly large from left to right. We informed participants that "0 (small bar) means 'not bad at all'" and "100 (big bar) means 'very bad'." Before beginning the task, participants were asked to select where 0 and 100 were represented on the scale. Participants were warned that they would have limited time to make their rating, and an image of a red square would appear at the bottom of the affect rating scale if they were too slow.

On average, participants failed to respond on only 2.3% of trials.

Participants completed 72 trials of this task (i.e., 18 trials per condition). To minimize any potential language-switching costs in auditory comprehension (Olson, 2017), trials were grouped into Spanish and English blocks such that participants completed all Spanish trials contiguously and all English trials contiguously. The order of these blocks was counterbalanced across participants (n = 123 [49.2%] had English followed by Spanish trials; n = 127 [50.8%] had Spanish followed by English trials). A non-pre-registered analysis of counterbalancing order showed that it did not affect primary results (see Supplemental Materials). Within the language blocks, the order of Describe and Reappraise trials was randomized. Trials were auto-advancing, and we included two attention checks randomly interspersed through the task in which participants had to drag a specified number from a list of numbers into a dropzone and submit.

Stimuli preparation.

Images were selected from the Open Affective Standardized Image Set (Kurdi et al., 2017). We sought to balance image content (e.g., "angry face," "sad face," "military personnel", etc.) across image lists (see Table S2 for stimuli sets). Additionally, to ensure all images were somewhat "negative", the normative valence rating for images were between 1 ("very negative") and 4 ("neutral") on a 1 to 7 scale, with 7 being "very positive." The selected 72 images were divided into 4 sets of 18 such that there were no statistically significant differences in valence or arousal ratings across the sets. T-tests for all pairwise comparisons produced ps > .837, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Table S3 for values).

Next, two members of the research team (one of whom is a Spanish-English bilingual) generated factual descriptions and reappraisals for each image in English, then translated them into Spanish, producing four "captions" per image: English description, English reappraisal, Spanish description, and Spanish reappraisal. We also aimed to ensure that the lists were balanced in terms of these captions. Using the National Research Council Canada Valence, Arousal, and Dominance Lexicon (Mohammad, 2018), valence and arousal ratings were calculated for each caption by averaging over the valence and arousal scores of all the words in the caption that were present in the lexicon. We used 1231 English and Spanish words in these captions (including conjugations and plurals), and 913 of these words (i.e., 74.17%) were present in the lexicon. We revised captions to produce final versions such that for both Describe and Reappraise captions, all comparisons across sets both within languages (e.g., English Describe set 1 versus English Describe set 2) and across languages (e.g., English Reappraise set 1 versus Spanish Reappraise set 1) did not show significant differences in valence and arousal ratings. All t-tests produced ps > .130, and all 95% confidence intervals included zero, indicating no reliable differences between sets (see Tables S2 and S4 for details). To ensure that translations were high quality, they were reviewed and edited by two Spanish-English bilingual speakers, one of whom is a high school Spanish teacher credited in the Acknowledgements. Just as for the vocabulary task, all captions were recorded by a bilingual co-author who identifies as a woman.

To ensure that condition differences were attributable to our task conditions (and not to specific images or audio recordings), the 4 sets of images were counterbalanced in their assignment to the 4 conditions (English Describe, English Reappraise, Spanish Describe, and Spanish Reappraise) across participants. For instance, one participant viewed Image Set A with English descriptions, while another participant received Image Set A with Spanish reappraisals, and so on. A pilot version of the study was conducted on Prolific with a separate sample of 12 participants to ensure that reappraisals produced lower negative affect than descriptions across both languages before proceeding to collect data for the main study, F(1, 715.20) = 73.16, p < .001, ηp² = .09, 90% CI [.06, .13].

Questionnaires. After the task, participants completed a set of questionnaires in English assessing demographics and relevant traits that could moderate our effects.

Bilingual Switching Questionnaire.

This questionnaire included 12 items assessing tendencies to switch languages (Rodriguez-Fornells et al., 2012). Although the scale was developed to examine switching between Spanish and Catalan, we adapted the items to investigate switching between Spanish and English. Items were answered on 5-point scales (1 = never,5 = always). The questionnaire had four subscales that showed weak to strong reliability in our sample: (i) L1 Spanish to L2 English switching (α = .54; e.g., "without intending to, I sometimes produce the English word faster when I am speaking in Spanish"), (ii) L2 English to L1 Spanish switching (α = .68; e.g., "when I cannot recall a word in Spanish, I tend to immediately produce it in English"), (iii) contextual switching (α = .80; e.g., "there are situations in which I always switch between the two languages"), and (iv) unintended switching (α = .67; e.g., "it is difficult for me to control the language switches I introduce during a conversation").

Emotion Regulation Questionnaire -cognitive reappraisal subscale.

We administered the 6-item cognitive reappraisal subscale of the Emotion Regulation Questionnaire (Gross & John, 2003). Participants reported how typical it is for them to regulate emotions using cognitive reappraisal (e.g., "I control my emotions by changing the way I think about the situation I'm in"). Items were rated on 7-point scales (1 = strongly disagree,7 = strongly agree), and this subscale showed strong reliability in this study (α = .91).

Patient Health Questionnaire-8 (PHQ-8).

Participants completed this 8-item measure of depressive symptoms over the last two weeks (e.g., "feeling down, depressed, or hopeless;" Kroenke et al., 2009). Ratings were given on 4-point scales (0 = not at all,3 = nearly every day), and reliability was strong in this sample (α = .89).

Generalized Anxiety Disorder Questionnaire (GAD-7).

This questionnaire assessed 7 symptoms of generalized anxiety disorder over the last two weeks (e.g., "feeling nervous, anxious, or on edge;" Spitzer et al., 2006). Items were rated on the same scale as the PHQ-8, and reliability was also strong (α = .92).

Analyses and Hypotheses

Analyses and hypotheses were pre-registered (https://osf.io/yufqs) and conducted with the statistical program R, version 4.6.0 (R Core Team, 2026). The primary pre-registered outcome measures for each participant were their negative affect ratings averaged within the task's four conditions: Spanish Describe, Spanish Reappraise, English Describe, and English Reappraise. To analyze the main effects of Audio Content and Language and their interaction, we pre-registered using a 2 [Audio Content: Describe vs. Reappraise] × 2 [Language: Spanish vs.

English] within-subjects ANOVA, analyzing condition-level mean negative affect ratings. This ANOVA was conducted using the ezANOVA function from the "ez" package (Lawrence, 2026).

We pre-registered the following hypotheses: 1) Participants will report higher negative affect on Describe trials than on Reappraise trials, and 2) Participants will report higher negative affect on Spanish trials than on English trials. We had competing hypotheses for the interaction effect: 3a)

Participants may show greater social reappraisal efficacy in Spanish than in English, perhaps due to greater familiarity with emotion regulation in the language they learned first; 3b) Participants may show greater cognitive reappraisal efficacy in English than in Spanish, perhaps due to increased emotional distance (i.e., reduced intensity of emotions) in L2; 3c) No interaction may emerge, i.e., social reappraisal efficacy is not significantly different in Spanish versus English.

However, after conducting the study, we discovered that trial-level analyses of reappraisal tasks provide superior power to detect effects compared to analyses of condition-level averages.

To provide the strongest possible test of our research questions, we thus deviated from the pre-registration and performed ANOVA analyses via linear mixed-effects models on trial-level responses (i.e., treating each negative affect rating as individual data points without averaging them within conditions, and including both participant ID and image as random effects;

Stadthagen-González et al., 2019). We followed up significant findings with pairwise contrasts within each language or audio content condition. We report these trial-level analyses in the main text but report pre-registered analyses of condition means in the Supplemental Materials.

Results do not differ across these methods, although the main effect of Language was trending using condition-level means (p = .055) but significant using trial-level ratings (p = .003).

In addition to these primary analyses, we pre-registered exploratory analyses of questionnaire measures to test whether they moderated our results. We pre-registered testing measures of Spanish and English proficiency, age of English acquisition, self-reported language dominance, and code-switching as moderators. For thoroughness, we also analyzed Spanish age of acquisition, being a simultaneous bilingual (i.e., learning English concurrently with Spanish) versus a sequential bilingual (i.e., learning English after Spanish), trait cognitive reappraisal use, depression symptoms, and anxiety symptoms as moderators. Given the number of tests and the exploratory nature of these analyses, we applied p-value correction (Benjamini & Hochberg, 1995) using the p.adjust function in the "stats" package (R Core Team, 2026).

Mixed-effects models were fitted using the lmer function in the "lmerTest" package (Kuznetsova et al., 2017) and then passed through the anova function in the "stats" package (R Core Team, 2026) to yield final statistics. Effect sizes from ANOVA tests are reported as partial eta squared (ηp² ) calculated using the eta_squared function from the "effectsize" package (Ben-Shachar et al., 2020). It is appropriate to report 90% confidence intervals for conventional statistical reporting of ηp² , as F-tests are technically one-sided tests (Lakens, 2013). Some supplemental analyses report Cohen's ds and standardized betas, which were produced using the "effectsize" package (Ben-Shachar et al., 2020). Plots were created using the package "ggplot2" (Wickham, 2016) and the "introdataviz" package (Nordmann et al., 2022).

Transparency and Openness

We report how we determined our sample size, all exclusions, all manipulations, and all measures in the study. The methods, hypotheses, and analytic plan for this study were pre-registered prior to data collection (https://osf.io/yufqs/). Data and analytic code are publicly available (http://osf.io/kvhma). We note which analyses were exploratory and all deviations from pre-registration both in the main text and in Table S7 (following Willroth & Atherton, 2024).

Results

Primary Analyses of Task Conditions

A mixed-effects analysis of trial-level negative affect ratings indicated a significant main effect of Audio Content, F(1, 17262) = 1788.04, p < .001, ηp² = .09, 90% CI [.09, .10], such that participants reported greater negative affect on Describe trials compared to Reappraise trials (Figure 2). Follow-up analyses within English trials demonstrated higher negative affect for English Describe (M = 41.11, SD = 32.11) than English Reappraise (M = 26.16, SD = 27.21) trials, F(1, 8477.5) = 968.66, p < .001, ηp² = .10, 90% CI [.09, .11]. Similarly, within Spanish trials, we found higher negative affect on Spanish Describe (M = 41.70, SD = 31.82) than Spanish Reappraise (M = 27.54, SD = 28.01) trials, F(1, 8468.3) = 850.31, p < .001, ηp² = .09, 90% CI [.08, .10]. These results support our first hypothesis: Negative affect was higher on Describe trials than Reappraise trials, both overall and in each language.

We also observed a significant effect of Language, F(1, 17262) = 8.86, p = .003, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, .001]. Participants reported higher negative affect on Spanish trials (M = 34.62, SD = 30.80) than on English trials (M = 33.65, SD = 30.69). Although this main effect supported our second hypothesis that negative affect would be higher in L1 than L2, note that the size was extremely small (conditions differed by ~1 point on a 100-point scale), and this main effect was only trending in the pre-registered ANOVA analysis of condition-level means (p = .055, Supplemental Materials).

However, the critical test was an interaction between Language and Audio Content. We found no significant interaction between these factors, (F(1, 17262) = 1.02, p = .313), ηp² < .001, 90% CI [.00, < .001], indicating that the effect of Audio Content (i.e., Describe versus Reappraise) on participants' negative affect was not significantly different between the two languages. This result aligns with the null alternative for our third hypothesis (3c): Social reappraisal was not significantly more effective in one language compared to the other.

Exploratory Analyses of Potential Moderators

We tested whether 13 relevant constructs moderated the task effects described above:

English age of acquisition, English proficiency, Spanish age of acquisition, Spanish proficiency, Spanish versus English dominance, simultaneous versus sequential bilingualism, four indices of code-switching, cognitive reappraisal use, depression symptoms, and anxiety symptoms. None of these moderators yielded a significant three-way interaction with Audio Content and Language after correcting for multiple comparisons. Thus, there is no strong evidence that differences in reappraisal success across languages emerge when a specific factor is taken into account.

However, some three-way interactions emerged that did not survive correction for multiple comparisons -anxiety symptoms, L2 English to L1 Spanish code-switching, and contextual code-switching. In recognition of ongoing debates as to when alpha correction is suitable (Rubin, 2021), we present these results in the Supplemental Materials for interested readers. We caution against overinterpreting them given their weak strength and the number of tests conducted.

By contrast, four moderators showed significant interactions with Audio Content even after controlling for multiple comparisons, and a fifth moderator's interaction failed to survive correction. These interactions indicate that individuals who reported lower English proficiency, greater cognitive reappraisal use, greater English to Spanish code-switching, and greater contextual code-switching benefitted more from social reappraisal overall (and individuals with later English acquisition also showed this pattern, although this effect did not survive correction, see Supplemental Materials).

Input during the review process prompted us to consider prior work showing that differences in L1 and L2 emotion processing primarily emerge in sequential bilinguals or bilinguals who use L2 less than L1 (Davydova et al., 2026; Degner et al., 2012; Harris, 2004).

We thus conducted a non-pre-registered test of our research questions only in the 196 participants (78.4% of sample) who reported learning English after Spanish. Results of primary analyses above are identical in this subsample (main effects of Audio Content and Language but no interaction between them, see Supplemental Materials). Thus, results do not change when only analyzing "sequential" bilinguals.foot_2

Discussion Converging research suggests that bilinguals learn and experience emotions differently in their first and second languages, and recent research has started to explore how bilinguals regulate emotions differently across languages (Table 1). Here, we examined whether the language in which social reappraisal is delivered moderates its efficacy in reducing negative affect for U.S.-based Spanish-English bilingual women. Almost all participants identified as Hispanic/Latinx, granting important insight into social emotion regulation in this population.

Participants reported less emotional intensity overall in English than in Spanish, but the regulatory benefit of social reappraisals did not significantly differ across languages. Results were consistent when only analyzing sequential bilinguals, and there were no significant moderators of this effect after controlling for multiple comparisons across 13 variables assessing language background, bilingual experience, and mental health.

These findings have broad implications for theories of emotion, bilingualism, and cognition, and they guide translational initiatives to support emotions in this population.

Our study replicated previous work on the efficacy of social reappraisal in regulating negative emotions in women (Horn & Maercker, 2016; Sahi et al., 2021 Sahi et al., , 2025)). Pre-recorded reappraisals of negative images produced lower negative affect than descriptions of those images, regardless of language. This provides yet another point of evidence that cognitive reappraisal (which has traditionally been studied as an intrapersonal/solo process; Nook, Schleider, et al., 2017; Ochsner et al., 2002; Petrova & Gross, 2023) can indeed be effectively implemented and studied in a dyadic setting. Additionally, our results provide a novel extension of this body of work into a bilingual context, showing that people feel better about images when hearing reappraisals in either English or Spanish.

We also found that participants reported higher negative affect on Spanish trials than on English trials. This finding extends past research on reduced emotional resonance in L2 (Davydova et al., 2026; Dewaele, 2004; Dylman & Bjärtå, 2019; García-Palacios et al., 2018; Harris, 2004; Lau et al., 2026; Sheikh & Titone, 2016) to social emotion regulation tasks. In particular, this result converges with those of Dylman & Bjärtå (2019) who showed that the purposeful switch from L1 to L2 led to lower psychological distress in Swedish-English participants as well as Lau et al. (2026) who found that people choose to discuss embarrassing topics in L2 to reduce emotional discomfort. More clinically, Pitta et al. (1978) observed that a Spanish-dominant female client in therapy was able to speak more objectively about her experiences when using English in later sessions of treatment (see also Altarriba & Santiago-Rivera, 1994; Santiago-Rivera et al., 2009). Together, these findings support the idea that emotions are overall "dulled" in English for Spanish-English bilinguals and suggest that switching to English may down-regulate emotions for this population. However, this effect was very small in size (~1 point on a 100-point scale) and only emerged as significant in non-pre-registered trial-level analyses. Furthermore, because we did not test both English-Spanish and Spanish-English bilinguals, we cannot determine whether this effect is due to switching to L2 or due to differences across English and Spanish themselves. Prior research shows that these languages differ in their encoding of psychological distance and emotion (Dodds et al., 2015; Guttfreund, 1990; Holmes et al., 2024; Llabre, 2021; Mulvaney-Day et al., 2007; Salvador et al., 2022). Thus, it is important to not overinterpret or overgeneralize this effect beyond the population we sampled here.

Critically, the primary research question we tested was whether social reappraisals were more or less beneficial in reducing negative emotion when offered in English versus in Spanish.

Based on prior work, we posited competing hypotheses for this research question. Interestingly, results supported our third pre-registered alternative hypothesis: Regulation efficacy did not significantly differ between English and Spanish. The down-regulating impact of social reappraisals was neither more nor less effective across languages. This result aligns with another recent study showing that the association between emotion words and facial expressions does not significantly differ across L1 and L2 (Champoux-Larsson & Nook, 2024).

However, it is crucial to contextualize these results as emerging within the specific sample we collected here. Although Spanish was a first learned language for all participants, most participants started learning English at a very early age (> 90% by age 9), considered English their dominant language (82.4%), and reported equal or higher English proficiency than Spanish proficiency (86.8%). Around one fifth were "simultaneous" bilinguals, who reported learning English simultaneously with Spanish (21.6%). Analyses testing whether language history variables moderated our effects did not suggest that language influenced reappraisal efficacy for only some participants, and the effect did not emerge just in sequential bilinguals.

Thus, this null effect seems relatively robust in the face of potential moderating factors, and we do not have evidence that convincingly reveals boundary conditions.

Indeed, our thorough test of potential moderators revealed that some were associated with enhanced reappraisal success overall, but none showed three-way interactions that survived correction for multiple comparisons. As one might expect, greater trait use of reappraisal was associated with greater regulatory benefits in this task, and curiously, participants with lower English proficiency also benefitted more. Given past research on the potential for language switching (or code-switching) from L1 to L2 to be a tool for down-regulation of negative emotions (Dylman & Bjärtåhttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?broken=bc0NR8 2019; Lau et al., 2026) , we asked whether participants' code-switching tendencies (Rodriguez-Fornells et al., 2012) affected reappraisal efficacy.

Supplemental analyses showed that two facets of code-switching (L2 English to L1 Spanish switching and contextual switching) were related to greater reappraisal efficacy, and although three-way interactions emerged, they did not survive correction for multiple comparisons.

Anxiety symptoms also showed a three-way interaction that did not survive correction. Thus, these moderation analyses identify some interesting factors that may enhance social reappraisal overall, but the weak nature of the three-way interactions (.01 ≤ ps uncorrected ≤ .05) does not provide compelling evidence that differences in reappraisal success across languages may emerge in specific populations. Nonetheless, these results highlight the need for additional research, especially in samples that can provide well-powered tests of these interactions. Furthermore, collecting data on the socio-emotional contexts in which participants code-switch to Spanish or English (e.g., switching more often to Spanish when feeling anger or to English when discussing emotions about school) could clarify these effects.

The conclusions of the study should be understood as primarily applying to U.S.

Spanish-English bilinguals with high fluency in both languages and overall dominance in English. Indeed, reviewing Table 1 demonstrates that our sample is similar to many others who use "balanced" bilinguals highly proficient in both L1 and L2, but it is one of few where L2 proficiency is higher than L1 proficiency. As mentioned in the introduction, studies find that bilinguals with an early age of L2 acquisition, high levels of L2 proficiency, and/or frequent L2 use and immersion show similar levels of emotional reactivity to stimuli in both languages (Davydova et al., 2026; Degner et al., 2012; Harris, 2004; Sutton et al., 2007). Our results align with these prior findings, indicating that they are likely confined to populations with language-learning characteristics similar to our sample, an important constraint on generalizability. Furthermore, as noted above, because we did not test both English-Spanish and Spanish-English bilinguals, we cannot rule out the possibility that a language effect masks L1-L2 reappraisal differences. For instance, reappraisal may be more effective in Spanish but more difficult in one's L1, and these effects may cancel each other here. In fact, Spanish has particular verbal structures that may facilitate regulation, like more positive words and unique means for connoting psychological distance (Dodds et al., 2015; Guttfreund, 1990; Holmes et al., 2024; Llabre, 2021; Mulvaney-Day et al., 2007; Salvador et al., 2022). Thus, more data are needed to test the generalizability of these effects across distinct populations, potentially borrowing cross-language approaches used in prior work (e.g., Keysar et al., 2012; Lau et al., 2026).

This study had several strengths. We adapted an established laboratory task for assessing emotional reactivity and reappraisal (Sahi et al., 2021 (Sahi et al., , 2025)), providing a tightly controlled test of our research question. Additionally, we innovated on these prior methods by developing a new version of the task that can directly compare the impact of speaking multiple languages on emotional experiences. Methods and hypotheses were pre-registered prior to launching the study, and the sample size was robust, even though it is a highly specific sample underrepresented in psychological research (Haack et al., 2014; Liang et al., 2009; Pedersen et al., 2022). Relatedly, we implemented robust screening procedures to ensure that participants met our strict inclusion criteria and were sufficiently fluent to engage with the task in both languages.

Nonetheless, several design decisions should be kept in mind when interpreting our results. First, although controlled experiments support high internal validity, their artificial nature can render them dissimilar to participants' everyday experiences. Images were static and did not robustly sample the diverse settings of one's natural life (e.g., school, work, friends, relationships, etc.). Additionally, the person delivering the social reappraisal was a stranger, whereas people often seek out friends and family for social support (Wellman & Wortley, 1990).

Testing more naturalistic stimuli and recruiting pairs of bilingual friends would improve the ecological validity of these results (Sahi et al., 2021 (Sahi et al., , 2025)). Second, because we controlled the language of the social reappraisal on each trial, this prevented participants from switching across languages. Allowing for more spontaneous switching between languages could reveal more naturalistic relations between language choice and emotion regulation. For example, studies could have bilingual participants spontaneously reappraise with a bilingual friend or include cross-language trials where the negative stimuli are experienced in one language and the reappraisals are delivered in another. Third, we did not ask how often participants tended to think in each language, which could reflect their habitual use of reappraisal in either language. Greater practice implementing reappraisal in L1 or L2 should be assessed in future research. Fourth, our pre-registered inclusion criteria led our study to mix "simultaneous" and "sequential" bilinguals.

This essentially means that for approximately 20% of the sample, English was not truly L2 but rather a second L1. Although we find identical results just when analyzing sequential bilinguals (n = 196), future research should specifically target this factor to clarify effects in each group.

Fifth, our study did not examine the influence of cultural dimensions (e.g., the extent to which participants identified with Spanish-speaking or English-speaking cultural groups or the strength of their individualistic versus collectivistic self-construals; Cross et al., 2011; Markus & Kitayama, 2010; Vignoles et al., 2016). We know that cultures differ in whether they encourage emotional expression, suppression, or reappraisal (Ford & Mauss, 2015; Liddell & Williams, 2019; Matsumoto, Yoo, et al., 2008; Mesquita & Frijda, 1992). Thus, language may influence regulation in bilingualism via cultural processes. Furthermore, given bilinguals' tendency to compartmentalize L1 and L2 based on context (e.g., L1 is dominant at home or with friends versus L2 for school and work; Boyer, 2012; Chen et al., 2012; Harris et al., 2003; Williams et al., 2020), the impact of language on reappraisal efficacy may be context-dependent: L1 may benefit reappraisal in personal relationships, whereas L2 may benefit reappraisal in workplace conflicts. Thus, future studies should ask participants their level of identification with the culture associated with each language, where they place themselves on relevant cultural dimensions, and in what social situations (school, work, family, friends, etc.) they tend to regulate using each language. Examining these factors could provide a much more nuanced view of when and why language influences regulatory success, with specific attention to culture. Sixth, this study only examined cognitive reappraisal, but reappraisal is just one of many regulatory strategies people use on their own and with others. In fact, studies show it is the 2 nd -3 rd most common form of social emotion regulation and the 16 th most common form of non-social regulation (Heiy & Cheavens, 2014; Liu et al., 2021). Thus, examining how linguistic contexts shape other forms of interpersonal emotion regulation (e.g., validation, problem solving, capitalization, co-rumination, etc.) is important for a full treatment of this research question.

Finally, our findings have implications for translational applications of emotion regulation research in social support settings for U.S.-based Spanish-English bilinguals, such as psychotherapy, parenting, and education. Fortunately, we found that the choice of providing reappraisals in either Spanish or English did not drastically affect outcomes for this population.

That said, future studies should first test this effect using more naturalistic paradigms that engage dyads who share a significant relationship before drawing this conclusion at a broad level.

Analyzing therapy transcripts (Abdou et al., 2025; Malgaroli et al., 2023; Nook et al., 2022; Sahi et al., in revision; Vartiainen et al., 2026) from bilingual client-therapist pairs would also provide a more naturalistic test of how bilingual individuals use language to regulate emotions in everyday life. In addition, testing these questions in different genders (men, mixed-gender dyads, or non-binary individuals), bilingual populations (e.g., English-Spanish bilinguals or different language pairs like Chinese and English), and ages (e.g., adult parent with young child versus older adult with adult child) would help establish the generalizability or specificity of these results. The present study has laid a foundation for extending social emotion regulation research to bilingual samples. This work can inform social support interventions for bilinguals and advance theories that explain how language shapes emotional experiences and regulation.

Constraints on Generality

Much of the discussion above is dedicated to considering the limitations of our findings for drawing inferences about other samples and contexts. We conclude with a brief summary of the most important points on this topic. This study recruited Spanish-English bilingual women who learned Spanish concurrently with or prior to English, lived in the United States, were highly proficient in both Spanish and English, and were ~100% Hispanic/Latinx-identifying.

Prior work establishes that language learning history (Davydova et al., 2026; Degner et al., 2012; Harris, 2004) and the specific languages one uses (Llabre, 2021) can influence emotion processes. Thus, inferences about the significant effect of language on emotion and the null effect of language on emotion regulation efficacy should be drawn only in relation to the population of bilinguals we sample here. Additional research is needed before we can conclude whether these patterns generalize when (i) samples include genders besides women, (ii) different languages are examined (e.g., Chinese or German), (iii) our specific L1 and L2 are reversed (i.e., English-Spanish bilinguals), (iv) the population is immersed in Spanish rather than English, (v) sampling is constrained only to sequential bilinguals, and (vi) proficiencies in either language are lower than the generally high proficiencies exhibited by our sample. Region-of-interest analyses again showed that "threat appraisal regions" were less responsive in fear conditioning task in L2 compared to L1, but this occurred more strongly for unbalanced (L1 dominant) than balanced bilinguals.

Self-reported dominant language

English n = 206 (82.4%) For each trial of the emotion regulation task, participants saw a negative image and heard an audio recording for 6 seconds. They then gave a negative affect rating within 4 seconds. The content of the audio clip determined whether it was an English Describe, English Reappraise, Spanish Describe, or Spanish Reappraise trial. To minimize the burden of switching languages, trials were ordered in language blocks (i.e., they completed all English trials contiguously and all Spanish trials contiguously). Describe and Reappraise trials were intermixed randomly within each block, and the order of Spanish vs. English blocks was counterbalanced across participants. Four lists of images from the OASIS stimuli set (Kurdi et al., 2017) were created with matched normative valence and arousal ratings. Narrations for all four conditions were created for all images, and steps were taken to ensure these were also matched on valence and arousal to prevent confounding. Assignment of image list to condition was counterbalanced across participants.

Figure 2. Negative affect ratings across each condition.

An ANOVA revealed significant main effects of both Audio Content and Language on participants' negative affect ratings. Participants reported higher negative affect on Describe trials (dark grey) compared to Reappraise trials (light grey), and lower negative affect on English trials than Spanish trials. The interaction was not significant, suggesting that language did not moderate the benefits of reappraisal. Black dots represent condition means, and error bars represent 95% confidence intervals.

V. Pre-registered Analyses of Condition-Level Averages

As we report in the main text, we pre-registered analyzing condition-level means using a standard 2 × 2 ANOVA. However, after completing the study, we discovered that mixed-effects analyses of trial-level responses (with random effects for image and participant) provide more sensitive tests than ANOVAs. To provide the most sensitive test possible of our research questions, we report trial-level analyses in the main text and condition-level analyses here.

When analyzing condition means for each participant, an ANOVA returned a significant main effect of Audio Content on participants' negative affect, F(1, 249) = 374.59, p < .001, ηp² = .60, 90% CI [.54, .65], such that participants reported a higher negative affect on Describe trials than on Reappraise trials. Follow-up t-tests demonstrated significant differences between English Describe and English Reappraise conditions, t(249) = 17.85, p < .001, d = 1.13, 95% CI [0.97, 1.29], and between Spanish Describe and Spanish Reappraise conditions, t(249) = 17.69, p < .001, d = 1.12, 95% CI [0.96, 1.28]. There was no significant effect of Language in the condition-level ANOVA, though the result was on the border of significance, F(1, 249) = 3.72, p = .055, ηp² = .01, 90% CI [.00, .05]. This analysis also did not return a significant interaction between Content and Language, F(1, 249) = 1.85, p = .175, ηp² = .007, 90% CI [.00, .03].

VI. Exploratory Analyses of Potential Moderators

We pre-registered exploratory analyses testing whether a set of relevant constructs could moderate task effects: Spanish and English proficiency, age of L2 acquisition, self-reported language dominance, and code-switching (which has 4 subscales). For thoroughness, we utilized additional measures that we had collected that were relevant to this research question (L1 age of acquisition, whether participants were simultaneous bilinguals [reported learning English concurrently with Spanish] or sequential bilinguals [reported learning English after Spanish], trait use of cognitive reappraisal in daily life, depression symptoms, and anxiety symptoms).

Each moderator was independently added into a trial-level linear mixed-effects model to test for interactions with Language and Audio Content in explaining negative affect scores.

Because this led to a total of 13 analyses, we decided to apply Benjamini-Hochberg p-value correction (Benjamini & Hochberg, 1995) before interpreting results. Specifically, we extracted the p-values for each moderator's: main effect, interaction with Audio Content, interaction with Language, and three-way interaction with Audio Content and Language. We then corrected these p-values, working within each effect (i.e., all the moderator main effect p-values were corrected for the 13 tests of this main effect, then all the moderator × Audio Content p-values were corrected, etc.). Although we did not state this in the pre-registration, correction seems wise to protect against false positives emerging due to repeated tests. However, there is debate regarding when to correct for multiple corrections, with scholars disagreeing on whether correction should always, never, or sometimes be applied (Rubin, 2021). The similarity in research questions underlying these questions (i.e., does a moderator exist?) and the number of tests conducted motivated us to apply correction and caution against overinterpreting results that do not survive this correction. Nonetheless, for maximal transparency, we present both corrected and uncorrected p-values in Table S5, ANOVA statistics in Table S6, and visualizations of all results below, even those that did not pass correction. We thus present our observations and allow interested readers to draw their own conclusions about the potential role of these moderators, although we ourselves would discourage strong interpretation of results that do not survive correction. All uncorrected p-values that do not survive correction are.01 ≤ p ≤ .05 and their effects are extremely small (i.e., ηp² ≤ .001, save for one moderator main effect that is larger).

Focused studies with greater power for testing three-way interactions could provide more clarity on the significance, replicability, and size of these effects.

Two-Way Interactions with Audio Content.

We first begin with results that passed correction for multiple comparisons. Four moderators showed significant interactions with Audio Content that survived correction for multiple comparisons (English proficiency, English to Spanish code-switching, contextual code-switching, and trait cognitive reappraisal use), and a fifth moderator only showed a significant uncorrected interaction (English age of acquisition).

These effects are visualized in Figure S1 and statistics are provided in Table S6. An interaction with Audio Content indicates that the difference in negative affect across Describe and Reappraise trials was larger for some participants than others. In other words, these moderators influenced how strongly participants benefitted from social reappraisal in this task, with participants benefitting more if they were less proficient in English (and perhaps learned English Generalized Anxiety Disorder -7 (see Table S6 for statistics). To help interpret these three-way interactions, we calculated a "reappraisal success" variable for each participant, where we subtracted their average negative affect rating on Reappraise trials from their average negative affect rating on Describe trials, separately for English and Spanish trials. We could then visualize reappraisal success in each language as a function of these moderators (see Figure S2).

The code-switching patterns suggest that the tendency to switch from English to Spanish (Figure S2A) or to contextually switch when triggered by specific situations, topics, or environments (Figure S2B) is positively associated with reappraisal success in English but not Spanish. It is possible that participants at very low levels of these bilingual switching scales benefit slightly more from social reappraisal in Spanish than in English, and this effect may reverse at very high levels of bilingual switching (i.e., when code-switching is high, social reappraisal may be more effective in English than Spanish). A simple Language × Audio Content interaction also emerged in these models, suggesting that after accounting for these dimensions of code-switching, participants benefitted more from social reappraisal in English than Spanish.

Finally, a simple main effect of contextual switching emerged, suggesting that higher contextual switching was associated overall with higher negative affect ratings, regardless of trial condition.

Visualizing the uncorrected three-way interaction between anxiety symptoms, Language, and Audio Content suggests that participants with high anxiety benefitted more from social reappraisal in English than in Spanish (Figure S2C).

Figure S2. Visualizing Three-Way Interactions (Caution: None Survive Correction for Multiple Comparisons)

To transparently describe patterns we see in the data, we present reappraisal success (i.e., the difference between a participant's average negative affect rating for Describe vs. Reappraise trials) according to three moderators that showed significant (uncorrected) three-way interactions, even though none of them survived correction for multiple comparisons. These results suggest that social reappraisal may be more effective in English than Spanish when participants report A) high tendencies to code-switch from English to Spanish, B) high tendencies to contextually code switch in response to specific prompts or environments, or C) high anxiety symptoms.

IX. Deviations from Pre-registration

Deviations # Details Original Wording Deviation Description Reader Impact 1 Type Analysis Within the task, we will compute average ratings within each condition…. We will test our primary hypotheses by performing a 2 × 2 ANOVA to quantify how Language (L1 vs. L2) and Audio Content (Describe vs. Reappraise) factors affect negative affect ratings. Rather than averaging ratings into one value for each condition per participant, we instead conducted mixed-effects models that analyze ratings on each trial as the primary test of our research questions. We include both participant ID and image as random effects.

After conducting the study, we discovered that trial-level analyses of reappraisal tasks provide superior power to detect effects compared to analyses of condition-level averages.

Finding null interaction effects with either analytic approach should strengthen the conclusion of a null interaction between Language and Audio Content. However, readers should consider that the main effect of Language (overall lower ratings for English than Spanish trials) only emerges as significant in the non-pre-registered analytic approach. We provide results from both the revised and original analytic approach, so readers are aware of this change, and we discuss it explicitly in the main text.

Figure S1. Moderators that Interacted with Audio Content

Audio Content interacted with English age of acquisition (which did not survive correction for multiple comparisons), and four other moderators that did survive correction. Larger differences between negative affect ratings on Describe trials (black) than on Reappraise (grey) trials indicate greater benefit from social emotion regulation. Thus, the direction of these results suggest that participants experienced greater benefits from social regulation if they reported: A) acquiring English later in life, B) lower English proficiency, C) greater code-switching from English to Spanish on the Bilingual Switching Questionnaire, D) greater contextual switching (i.e., the tendency to code-switch between languages when triggered by a particular situation, topic, or environment, also assessed by the Bilingual Switching Questionnaire), and E) greater trait use of cognitive reappraisal (as measured by the Emotion Regulation Questionnaire).

Three-Way Interactions and Other Interactions.

All interactions described in this section failed correction for multiple comparisons and should be interpreted with caution. Three moderators showed uncorrected three-way interactions with Language and Audio Content. These include two subscales of the Bilingualism Switching Questionnaire (L2 English to L1 Spanish code-switching and contextual code-switching) and anxiety symptoms as measured by the

VII. Non-Pre-registered Exploratory Analysis Within Sequential Bilinguals

Input during the review process prompted us to consider prior work showing that L1 and L2 differences in emotion processes primarily emerge in sequential bilinguals and in bilinguals who use L2 less than L1 (Davydova et al., 2026; Degner et al., 2012; Harris, 2004). We thus tested if differences in regulation across Spanish and English may emerge specifically in a subsample of participants who reported learning English after Spanish. We split participants into "simultaneous bilinguals" (i.e., those who reported learning English at the same age as Spanish; n = 54) and "sequential bilinguals" (i.e., those who reported learning English after Spanish; n = 196). As reported in Table S5, adding this factor did not produce a significant three-way interaction. However, we wanted to ensure that the effect does not emerge when just analyzing the subsample of sequential bilinguals. We thus conducted a non-pre-registered analysis of our primary research question within the n = 196 sequential bilinguals.

This analysis returned main effects of both Audio Content, F(1, 13524) = 1432.96, p < .001, ηp² = .10, 90% CI [.09, .11], and Language, F(1, 13524) = 11.14, p < .001, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, .002], but no interaction between them, F(1, 13524) = 0.66, p = .416, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, < .001]. To provide a common-sense estimate of the impact of Language on reappraisal within this subsample, we computed a Cohen's d effect size for the difference in reappraisal efficacy across Spanish and English conditions. We computed participant-level mean differences in ratings for Spanish Describe and Spanish Reappraise trials, as well as participant-level mean differences in ratings for English Describe and English Reappraise trials, and then we measured the size of the within-subjects difference across these two estimates of "reappraisal success" in each language. This difference was very small (d = 0.07, 95% CI [-0.07, 0.21]), with reappraisal success numerically larger in English (M = 15.01) than Spanish (M = 14.31). Thus, if there is any effect within this subsample, it is extremely small (seven one-hundredths of a standard-deviation change within-persons) and would require 1,604 participants to detect with 80% power. Although this is a post-hoc test in just a subsample of participants (see footnote 3 in the primary article for a discussion of power), this result again aligns with hypothesis 3c: There is no compelling evidence that reappraisal success differs between L1 and L2 in Spanish-English bilinguals, even when restricting to sequential bilinguals only.

VIII. Non-Pre-registered Exploratory Analyses of Language Order Counterbalancing

We conducted a non-pre-registered analysis testing whether the order in which participants completed English versus Spanish blocks affected their responses. Recall that n = 123 participants (49.2%) had English followed by Spanish trials, and n = 127 participants (50.8%) had Spanish followed by English trials. Language block order was added into a linear mixed-effects model. We did not observe a three-way interaction with Language Block Order, F(1, 17259) = 0.24, p = .622, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [0.00, <.001], nor did we find an interaction between Language and Content after controlling for order counterbalancing, F(1, 17259) = 1.02, p = .311, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [0.00, < .001]. Thus, the main effects of Content and Language were not attributable to which language participants completed first. We did find an interaction between Audio Content and Language Block Order, F(1, 17259) = 7.68, p = .006, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [< .001, .001], such that participants appeared to benefit more from reappraisal if they did Spanish trials first (Describe vs. Reappraise difference: M = 15.49, SD = 12.06) rather than English trials first (Describe vs. Reappraise difference: M = 13.55, SD = 11.61) (Figure S4).

However, the lack of a three-way interaction implies that participants who completed Spanish trials first benefitted more from reappraisal in both English and Spanish.

Figure S4. Interaction of Content and Language Block Order

There was a significant interaction between Content and Language Block Order (i.e. English trials first or Spanish trials first); participants showed a greater difference in negative affect between Describe and Reappraise trials if they completed Spanish trials first. However, the lack of a three-way interaction of Content × Language × Language Block Order indicates that the main effects of Content and Language were not attributable to which language participants completed first. Black points indicate means, and confidence intervals are 95%.

Figure 1 :

Figure1: Task design. For each trial of the emotion regulation task, participants saw a negative image and heard an audio recording for 6 seconds. They then gave a negative affect rating within 4 seconds. The content of the audio clip determined whether it was an English Describe, English Reappraise, Spanish Describe, or Spanish Reappraise trial. To minimize the burden of switching languages, trials were ordered in language blocks (i.e., they completed all English trials contiguously and all Spanish trials contiguously). Describe and Reappraise trials were intermixed randomly within each block, and the order of Spanish vs. English blocks was counterbalanced across participants. Four lists of images from the OASIS stimuli set(Kurdi et al., 2017) were created with matched normative valence and arousal ratings. Narrations for all four conditions were created for all images, and steps were taken to ensure these were also matched on valence and arousal to prevent confounding. Assignment of image list to condition was counterbalanced across participants.

Table 1.

Characteristics of bilingual samples and findings of related studies investigating L1 versus L2 effects on emotion and physiology.

Table 2 :

Participant Demographics

Sutton et al. (2007) also found that Stroop responses did not significantly differ across L1 and L2 emotion words in a sample of Spanish-English bilinguals with strong English (L2) dominance. However, they did not test whether language learning history moderated this effect.

All exclusion criteria were pre-registered except for the last criterion (reporting different birth years across screener and main study). We added this for additional certainty that participants were attentive and honest.

Note that the sample size of sequential bilinguals(n = 196) used in this test is smaller than the N we pre-registered in our power analysis to have 80% power to detect effects as large as d = .20 (i.e., n = 198). That said, it is only 2 participants short from this target, and power analyses show that this sample grants 79.6% power to detect small effects (d = .20) and 80% power to detect effects as small as d = .201. Notably, the effect size we do observe for this effect (d = .07; a within-person shift of seven one-hundredths of a standard deviation) is far smaller than this effect size of interest, and the average difference in reappraisal success we observe is a numerical shift of 0.7 points on a one-hundred point scale. In fact, if the population effect is truly d = .07, that would require 1,604 participants to detect with 80% power.
